[02:40:19] Thank you. Good afternoon, members of the board. I'm Lisa Madron, and currently serving as the Chief Transformation Officer. And I'm here today with Robin Fontaine, our Budget Manager from Community Services. And then I will be turning over for the second part of the construction update to Matt and his team at FFM.

[02:40:42] I will be providing you a brief update on what the CRC, or the Crisis Receiving Center, is, what it was needed, where we started, and where we are now. Our vision for the Crisis Receiving Center was to have places where adults and youth could go who were experiencing a behavioral health crisis to get the help that they needed to immediately resolve the crisis and where appropriate to get connected to mental health and substance abuse services, which involved co-locating some of the programs offered by community services to the Crisis Receiving Center.

[02:41:18] Our considering a new way of providing crisis services in the community was very timely as Virginia was also looking at what constituted a robust crisis system and also found that the crisis now model was such a system as this model provided a place to call services that would go out to individuals in crisis and a place for individuals to go.

[02:41:39] In July of 2022, a national transition was made to 988 as the digital code for the crisis lifeline, and Virginia also established a regional crisis call line. Mobile crisis has been offered in our region for several years, and Virginia also expanded these services.

[02:41:59] What the significant gap in Prince William County, however, has been a place for individuals experiencing a behavioral health crisis to go and get immediate help. Hospitals have been the place individuals have gone but have not been able to meet the need, and if a psychiatric bed was located, it was often outside of the region for Prince William County residents.

[02:42:19] A crisis receiving center will fulfill the third part of this robust crisis system, as it is a place for those seeking help voluntarily or under a temporary detention order. The crisis receiving center offers immediate assessment and 23-hour observation and treatment.

[02:42:38] For those who may need longer treatment, residential crisis stabilization beds are on site. The Crisis Receiving Center also provides withdrawal management for those withdrawing from substances as the multidisciplinary team providing services includes medical staff.

[02:42:57] Community Services is contracting with Connections Health Solutions, an experienced national leader in crisis services to operate the Crisis Receiving Center for adults and the Crisis Receiving Center for youth. Connections has been working collaboratively with CS and Prince William County on the design and the construction of the CRC and has been instrumental and a strong partner in getting legislation changed this year to allow on-site medications and the use of seclusion at the facility.

[02:43:26] They have also established an on-site program team who is working to develop relationships and connections across our community. We first began this journey focused on starting the adult CRC with the funding provided through the county, but as you'll see, we're going to be able to address both the adult and the youth CRCs.

[02:43:47] Because the need is so great for a CRC, it may seem that this initiative may not be moving fast enough, but this is a huge initiative never done like this before in Virginia, and it really began as a directive three years ago.

[02:44:04] Approximately 16 months later, the funding was approved by the county board, which was thought to be for phase one or part of the adult CRC, eight 23-hour units and eight beds, rather than the 16 and 16. The request for proposal that we put out and awarded to Connections allowed us to move forward with our full adult CRC vision of the 16 and 16.

[02:44:28] In December of 2022, the Department of Behavioral Health and Developmental Services provided community services the funding toward part of the youth CRC. The full vision at the time was for eight 23-hour units and eight beds for youth.

[02:44:46] Just over a year ago, Prince William County purchased the Worth Avenue building, and we have the dedication ceremony there in what looked like a big warehouse, and it does not look like that today. A year ago, Community Services submitted an application to the state for ongoing funding needed to double the youth capacity to 16 and 16.

[02:45:09] This past March, I was notified by the department that we would be receiving the ongoing funding for youth in July. The General Assembly further assured and supported our funding, And we are very grateful for the support and the funding that will allow us to offer these very needed services to adults and to youth.

[02:45:33] This slide shows the estimate for full operations of the adult and the youth CRC with 16 23-hour observation units each for adults and 16 crisis stabilization beds each for adults and for youth.

[03:45:36] Meeting the goals that this board has set will not occur, will not happen, if renewable sources are not in fact approved. You are not overburdened with applications for renewable energy in Prince William County.

[03:46:13] It does identify uses that are industrial, for example, brewery and distilling facilities, industrial equipment sales, coal storage. There are 73 uses by right in the M1 zone, 27 by special use permit, for the purpose this board has set out to encourage development of heavy and intensive industrial processing, manufacturing, and storage.

[03:46:37] 45 uses in the M2 zone and 22 by special use permit, quote, for research and development centers, light industrial manufacturing, warehousing, wholesaling, and related office, and institutional uses. The M2, MT district, excuse me, which I wrote many years ago for a former member of the board, is to encourage development of heavy industrial uses, and in particular those which generate considerable truck and or heavy equipment traffic, which require access to more than one mode of transportation.

[03:47:08] A solar energy facility is permitted in all three of those industrial districts, but it's a use without noise, odors, glare, or traffic, hidden behind extensive screening with a finite life. It is the only use that is available in an industrial district that is also available in an agricultural district with a special use permit, heavily conditioned to the point that it will not be visible to its near neighbors or to the traveling public.
